and
(1,2)
is an
{\bf m}({\bf P}_{-};\alpha)
-pair.
For any
(\Delta^{\prime},P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime})\in\pi({\bf P})
, from the construction of
\pi
, the edges labeled
\alpha_{2}=\tau_{[1]}(p)
and
\alpha_{4}
are in
P^{\prime}
. Thus
(\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(p),P^{\prime}_{-},\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q))\in\pi({\bf P}_{-})
.

Case 2.2.2.
\widetilde{\beta}
crosses
\alpha_{4}
and ending at
q
. Then
m_{\eta_{\alpha}}(P_{-})=1
and thus
\sum m_{i}(P_{-})>0
. As
m(\Delta_{1}(p);\alpha)\geq 0
, we have
(\eta_{\alpha},\eta_{\alpha}+1)
is an
{\bf m}({\bf P}_{-};\alpha)
-pair. For any
(\Delta^{\prime},P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime})\in\pi({\bf P}_{-})
, from the construction of
\pi
, we have the edges labeled
\tau_{t-1}(q)=\alpha_{2}
and
\tau_{[1]}(q)=\alpha_{4}
of
G^{\prime}(\eta_{\alpha})
are in
P^{\prime}
. Thus
(\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(p),P^{\prime}_{-},\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q))\in\pi({\bf P}_{-})
.

Case 3.
m(\Delta_{1}(p);\alpha)<0,m(\Delta_{1}(q);\alpha)\geq 0
. This case is similar to case 2.

Case 4.
m(\Delta_{1}(p);\alpha),m(\Delta_{1}(q);\alpha)<0
. Thus we have
\phi_{\alpha}^{T^{o},q}(\Delta_{1}(q))=\{\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q)\}
and
\phi_{\alpha}^{T^{o},p}(\Delta_{1}(p))=\{\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(p)\}
.

If
\sum m_{i}(P_{-})\leq 0
, then
\varphi_{\alpha}^{T^{o}}(P_{-})=\{P^{\prime}_{-}\}
. Thus
\{(\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(p),P^{\prime}_{-},\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q))\}=\pi({\bf P}_{-})
.

If
\sum m_{i}(P_{-})>0
, then by Lemma
7.11
we see that
\tau_{[1]}(p),\tau_{[1]}(q)\in\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}\}
. For any
(\Delta^{\prime},P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime})\in\pi({\bf P}_{-})
, from the construction of
\pi
, we have the edges labeled
\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{4}
of
G^{\prime}(a)
are in
P^{\prime}
, where
a
is in some
{\bf m}({\bf P}_{-};\alpha)
-pair. Thus
(\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(p),P^{\prime}_{-},\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q))\in\pi({\bf P}_{-})
.

The proof is complete.

\square

Theorem 10.1
.

Under the
Assumption
, assume that
{\bf P}
covers
{\bf Q}
and related by
\tau
. Denote
{\bf P}(0,\cdots,0)={\bf P}^{\prime}
and
{\bf Q}(0,\cdots,0)={\bf Q}^{\prime}
.

(1)

If
\tau\neq\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
, then
{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
and related by
\tau
. Moreover, we have

\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q})=\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}).

(2)

If
\tau=\alpha
, then we have either

\bullet

{\bf P}^{\prime}={\bf Q}^{\prime}
or

\bullet

{\bf P}^{\prime}
is covered by
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
and related by
\alpha^{\prime}
with
\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q})=-\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}).

Proof.

(1) As
\tau\neq\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
, we have
{\bf m}({\bf P};\alpha)={\bf m}({\bf Q};\alpha)
.

We first consider the situation that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta})
. Then
{\bf P}=P
and
{\bf Q}=\mu_{G_{l}}P
. By Proposition
7.17
, there is a tile
G^{\prime}_{[l]}
of
G_{T^{\prime o},\widetilde{\beta}}
with diagonal labeled
\tau
such that any
P^{\prime}\in\varphi_{\alpha}^{T^{o}}(P)
can twist on
G^{\prime}_{[l]}
,
P^{\prime}>\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}(P^{\prime})
, and
\varphi_{\alpha}^{T^{o}}({\bf Q})=\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}(\varphi_{\alpha}^{T^{o}}(P))
. Thus
{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
and related by
\tau
. As
{\bf m}(P,\alpha)={\bf m}(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\alpha)
, we see that
\pi({\bf Q})=\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}(\pi(P))
. By Proposition
7.17
(1), we have
m^{\pm}(P,G_{l};\tau_{i_{l}})=m^{\pm}({\bf P}^{\prime},G^{\prime}_{[l]};\tau_{i_{l}})
and
n(G_{l}^{\pm};\tau_{i_{l}})=n(G^{\prime\pm}_{[l]};\tau_{i_{l}})
, thus
\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime})=\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q})
.

We then consider the situation that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
. Assume that
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
.

If
{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
, then by Proposition
7.17
there is a tile
G^{\prime}_{[l]}
of
G_{T^{\prime o},\widetilde{\beta}}
with diagonal labeled
\tau
such that

\pi(Q)=\{(\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}(P^{\prime}),\Delta^{\prime})\mid(P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})\in\pi({\bf P})\}
and
(P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})>(\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}(P^{\prime}),\Delta^{\prime})
. In particular,
{\bf P}^{\prime}>{\bf Q}^{\prime}
. Next, we show
{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
. Otherwise, by Lemma
4.4
we have
G^{\prime}_{[l]}
is the last tile of
G_{T^{\prime o},\widetilde{\beta}}
and
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q)
is the first triangle incidents to
q
in
T^{\prime o}
. As
\tau\neq\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
, we see that
G_{l}
is the last tile of
G_{T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}}
and
m(\Delta^{\prime};\alpha)=0
and thus
\Delta_{j}(q)=\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta_{1}(q)
is the first triangle incidents to
q
in
T^{o}
. By Lemma
4.4
,
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
does not cover
{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
, a contradiction. Therefore,
{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
. Assume that
{\bf P}^{\prime}=(P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
. Then
{\bf Q}^{\prime}=(\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}(P^{\prime}),\Delta^{\prime})
. From the previous case, we have
\Omega(P,\mu_{G_{l}}P)=\Omega(P^{\prime},\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}(P^{\prime}))
. By Lemma
8.10
, we see that
\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime})=\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q})
.

If
{\bf Q}=(P,\Delta_{j-1}(q))
, as
\tau\neq\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
, we have
m(\Delta_{j-1}(q);\alpha)=m(\Delta_{j}(q);\alpha)=0
. Thus
{\bf P}^{\prime}=(P^{\prime},\Delta_{j}(q))
for some
P^{\prime}
and
{\bf Q}^{\prime}=(P^{\prime},\Delta_{j-1}(q))
. In case neither
\Delta_{j-1}(q)
nor
\Delta_{j}(q)
is the first triangle incident to
q
in
T^{\prime o}
, by Lemma
4.5

{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
. In case either
\Delta_{j-1}(q)
or
\Delta_{j}(q)
is the first triangle incident to
q
in
T^{\prime o}
, as
\tau=\tau_{j-1}(q)\neq\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
, we see the last tiles of
G_{T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}}
and
G_{T^{\prime o},\widetilde{\beta}}
are the same and
E_{1}(p)\in P
iff
E^{\prime}_{1}(p)\in P^{\prime}
. As
{\bf P}
coves
{\bf Q}
, by Lemma
4.5
we have
{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
. By Lemma
7.16
, we have
\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime})=\Omega(P,Q)
.

The situation that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(p,q)})
is similar to the case
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
.

(2) As
\tau=\alpha
, we have
{\bf m}({\bf P};\alpha)={\bf m}({\bf Q};\alpha)
. Suppose that
{\bf P}^{\prime}\neq{\bf Q}^{\prime}
.

We first consider the situation that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta})
. Then
{\bf P}=P
and
{\bf Q}=\mu_{G_{l}}P
. By Proposition
7.17
(2), we have
m_{k}(P)=-1
and
G_{l}=G(k)
for some
k
.
As
{\bf P}^{\prime}\neq{\bf Q}^{\prime}
,
k
is in some
{\bf m}(P,\alpha)={\bf m}(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\alpha)
-pair. Assume that
(k,k^{\prime})
is an
{\bf m}(P,\alpha)
-pair, then
m_{k^{\prime}}(\mu_{G_{l}}P)=m_{k^{\prime}}(P)=1
. Thus the labels of
{\bf P}^{\prime}\cap edge(G^{\prime}(k^{\prime}))
and
P\cap edge(G(k))
coincide, the labels of
{\bf Q}^{\prime}\cap edge(G^{\prime}(k^{\prime}))
and
\mu_{G_{l}}P\cap edge(G(k))
coincide. Therefore
{\bf Q}^{\prime}=\mu_{G^{\prime}(k^{\prime})}{\bf P}^{\prime}
and
{\bf Q}^{\prime}>{\bf P}^{\prime}
. Denote
G^{\prime}_{[l]}=G^{\prime}(k^{\prime})
. We have

m^{+}(P,G_{l};\alpha)-n(G^{+}_{l};\alpha)=\sum_{i>k}m_{i}(P),\hskip 14.22636ptm^{-}(P,G_{l};\alpha)-n(G^{-}_{l};\alpha)=\sum_{i<k}m_{i}(P),

m^{+}({\bf P}^{\prime},G^{\prime}_{[l]};\alpha^{\prime})-n(G^{\prime+}_{[l]};\alpha^{\prime})=\sum_{i>k^{\prime}}m_{i}({\bf P}^{\prime}),\hskip 14.22636ptm^{-}({\bf P}^{\prime},G^{\prime}_{[l]};\alpha^{\prime})-n({\bf G}^{\prime-}_{[l]};\alpha^{\prime})=\sum_{i<k^{\prime}}m_{i}(P^{\prime}).

It follows that

\Omega(P,\mu_{G_{l}}P)=d(\alpha)(\sum_{i>k}m_{i}(P)-\sum_{i<k}m_{i}(P)),\hskip 14.22636pt\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime})=d(\alpha^{\prime})(\sum_{i>k^{\prime}}m_{i}({\bf P}^{\prime})-\sum_{i<k^{\prime}}m_{i}({\bf P}^{\prime})).

As
{\bf m}(P,\alpha)=-{\bf m}({\bf P}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
and
(k,k^{\prime})
is an
{\bf m}(P,\alpha)
-pair, we obtain
\Omega(P,\mu_{G_{l}}P)=-\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime})
.

We then consider the situation that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
. Assume that
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
.

If
{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
, then by Proposition
7.17
(2) we have
m_{k}({\bf P})=-1
and
G_{l}=G(k)
for some
k
.
As
{\bf P}^{\prime}\neq{\bf Q}^{\prime}
,
k
is in some
{\bf m}({\bf P},\alpha)
-pair. Assume that
(k,k^{\prime})
is an
{\bf m}({\bf P},\alpha)
-pair, then
m_{k^{\prime}}({\bf Q})=m_{k^{\prime}}({\bf P})=1
. Assume
{\bf P}^{\prime}=(P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
.

In case
k^{\prime}\neq\eta_{\alpha}+1
, we have
{\bf Q}^{\prime}=(\mu_{G^{\prime}(k^{\prime})}(P^{\prime}),\Delta^{\prime})>{\bf P}^{\prime}
. We claim
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf P}^{\prime}
. Otherwise, by Lemma
4.4

G^{\prime}(k^{\prime})
is the last tile of
G_{T^{\prime o},\widetilde{\beta}}
and
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q)
. It follows
k^{\prime}=\eta_{\alpha}
and
m_{\eta_{\alpha}}({\bf P}^{\prime})=-1,m_{\eta_{\alpha}+1}({\bf P}^{\prime})=1
. Thus
(k^{\prime}=\eta_{\alpha},\eta_{\alpha}+1)
is an
{\bf m}({\bf P},\alpha)
-pair, contradicts to
(k,k^{\prime})
is an
{\bf m}({\bf P},\alpha)
-pair. Therefore,
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf P}^{\prime}
. Similar to the case
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta})
, we have
\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q})=-\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime})
.

In case
k^{\prime}=\eta_{\alpha}+1
, we have
m_{\eta_{\alpha}+1}({\bf P})=1
, we may assume that
\tau_{j-1}(q)=\alpha_{1},\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha_{4}
. Thus,
\Delta^{\prime}=\{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
and
{\bf Q}^{\prime}=(P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime})
, where
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=\{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}\}
, see Figure
18
. We claim
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf P}^{\prime}
. Otherwise, by Lemma
4.5
, we have either
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q),E_{2}^{\prime}(q)\in P^{\prime}
or
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q),E_{1}^{\prime}(q)\in P^{\prime}
. In both cases, we have
\Delta_{j}(q)=\Delta_{1}(q)
and
\tau_{i_{c}}=\alpha
. If
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q),E_{2}^{\prime}(q)\in P^{\prime}
, then
E^{\prime}_{2}(q)
is labeled
\alpha^{\prime}
. If
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=\Delta^{\prime}_{1}(q),E_{1}^{\prime}(q)\in P^{\prime}
, then
E^{\prime}_{1}(q)
is labeled
\alpha^{\prime}
. Thus
m_{\eta_{\alpha}}({\bf P^{\prime}})=1
and
m_{\eta_{\alpha}}({\bf P})=-1
. It follows that
G_{l}=G_{c}
is the last tile. It contradicts to
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
covers
{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
. Therefore,
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf P}^{\prime}
. Similar to the case
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta})
, we have
\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q})=-\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime})
.

Figure 18.

If
{\bf Q}=(P,\Delta_{j-1}(q))
, then
m_{\eta_{\alpha}+1}({\bf P})=-1
. As
{\bf P}^{\prime}\neq{\bf Q}^{\prime}
,
\eta_{\alpha}+1
is in some
{\bf m}({\bf P},\alpha)
-pair. Assume that
(k,\eta_{\alpha}+1)
is an
{\bf m}({\bf P},\alpha)
-pair and
{\bf P}^{\prime}=(P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
. Then
{\bf Q}^{\prime}=(\mu_{G^{\prime}(k^{\prime})}P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
. Dual to the case
k^{\prime}=\eta_{\alpha}+1
for
{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
, we can prove
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf P}^{\prime}
and
\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q})=-\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime})
.

The situation that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(p,q)})
is similar to the case
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
.

\square

Theorem 10.2
.

Under the
Assumption
, let
{\bf P}=P,(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
or
(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
. Assume that
{\bf P}
covers some
{\bf Q}
and related by
\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
with
\alpha_{k}\neq\alpha_{k-1},\alpha_{k+1}
. Suppose that all
\alpha
-mutable edges of
P
are labeled
\alpha_{k-1}
or
\alpha_{k+1}
.

(1)

Assume that
k\in\{1,3\}
, denote
{\bf P}(0,\cdots,0)={\bf P}^{\prime}
and
{\bf Q}(0,\cdots,0)={\bf Q}^{\prime}
. Then except for the following cases,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha
and
(P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
{\bf P}
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{i}(p)\neq\alpha,\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha
and
(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
{\bf P}
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{j}(q)\neq\alpha,\tau_{i}(p)=\alpha
and
(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
covers
{\bf P}
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{i}(p)=\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha
,
(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
covers
{\bf P}
and
(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha
and
(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
{\bf P}
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j+1}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{i}(p)=\alpha
and
(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
{\bf P}
,

we have
{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
and related by
\alpha_{k}
. Moreover, we have
{\bf P}={\bf P}^{\prime}(1,\cdots,1)
covers
{\bf Q}={\bf Q}^{\prime}(1,\cdots,1)
and related by
\alpha_{k}
, and

\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q}_{1})=\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}).

(2)

Assume that
k\in\{2,4\}
, denote
{\bf P}(1,\cdots,1)={\bf P}^{\prime}
and
{\bf Q}(1,\cdots,1)={\bf Q}^{\prime}
. Then except for the following cases,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{j-1}(q)=\alpha
and
{\bf P}
covers
(P,\Delta_{j-1}(q))
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{i-1}(p)\neq\alpha,\tau_{j-1}(q)=\alpha
and
{\bf P}
covers
(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j-1}(q))
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{j-1}(q)\neq\alpha,\tau_{i-1}(p)=\alpha
and
{\bf P}
covers
(\Delta_{i-1}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{i-1}(p)=\tau_{j-1}(q)=\alpha
,
{\bf P}
covers
(\Delta_{i-1}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
and
(\Delta_{i-1}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
covers
(\Delta_{i-1}(p),P,\Delta_{j-1}(q))
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{j-1}(q)=\alpha
and
{\bf P}
covers
(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j-1}(q))
,

\bullet

{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j+1}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{i-1}(p)=\alpha
and
{\bf P}
covers
(\Delta_{i-1}(p),P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
,

we have
{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
and related by
\alpha_{k}
. Moreover, assume that
{\bf P}_{1}={\bf P}^{\prime}(0,\cdots,0),{\bf Q}_{1}={\bf Q}^{\prime}(0,\cdots,0)
, then
{\bf P}_{1}
covers
{\bf Q}_{1}
and related by
\alpha_{k}
, and

\Omega({\bf P}_{1},{\bf Q}_{1})=\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}).

Proof.

We shall prove (1) as (2) can be proved similarly.

We first consider the situation that
{\bf P}=P
. Then
{\bf Q}=\mu_{G_{l}}P
for some tile
G_{l}
. As all the
\alpha
-mutable edges in
P
are labeled
\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{4}
, we see that all the
\alpha
-mutable edges in
\mu_{G_{l}}P
and
\alpha^{\prime}
-mutable edges in
{\bf P}^{\prime}
are labeled
\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{4}
. By Proposition
7.17
(3), there is a tile
G^{\prime}_{[l]}
of
G_{T^{\prime o},\widetilde{\beta}}
with diagonal labeled
\alpha_{1}
such that
{\bf P}^{\prime}
can twist on
G^{\prime}_{[l]}
and
{\bf P}^{\prime}>\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}{\bf P}^{\prime}\in\varphi_{\alpha}^{T^{o}}(\mu_{G_{l}}P)
. It follows that all the
\alpha^{\prime}
-mutable edges in
\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}{\bf P}^{\prime}
are labeled
\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{4}
. Therefore, we have
\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}{\bf P}^{\prime}\in\pi(\mu_{G_{l}}P)
and
\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}{\bf P}^{\prime}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P)(0,\cdots,0),(\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}{\bf P}^{\prime})(1,\cdots,1)=\mu_{G_{l}}(P)
. By Proposition
7.17
(3), we have
\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime};G^{\prime}_{[l]})=\Omega(P;G_{l})
.

We then consider the situation that
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
.

Case 1.
{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
. As the case that
\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha
and
(P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
{\bf P}
is excepted, we shall consider the following cases:

Case 1.1.
\tau_{j}(q)\neq\alpha
;
Case 1.2.
\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha
but
(P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
does not cover
{\bf P}
.

In Case 1.1, we have either
m(\Delta_{j}(q);\alpha)\geq 0
or
m(\Delta_{j}(q);\alpha)=-1
with
\tau_{[j]}(q)\in\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}\}
, the case can be proved similar to the case that
{\bf P}=P
.

In Case 1.2, by Lemma
4.9
we have
G_{l}\neq G_{c}
and
(\eta_{\alpha},\eta_{\alpha}+1)
is an
{\bf m}({\bf P};\alpha)
and
{\bf m}({\bf Q};\alpha)
-pair. Assume that
{\bf P}^{\prime}=(P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
. Thus
P^{\prime}\cap edge(G^{\prime}(\eta_{\alpha}))
is labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}
. By Proposition
7.17
(3), there is a tile
G^{\prime}_{[l]}
of
G_{T^{\prime o},\widetilde{\beta}}
with diagonal labeled
\alpha_{1}
such that
{\bf P}^{\prime}
can twist on
G^{\prime}_{[l]}
and
{\bf P}^{\prime}>\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}{\bf P}^{\prime}\in\varphi_{\alpha}^{T^{o}}(\mu_{G_{l}}P)
. Since
G_{l}\neq G_{c}
, we have
\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}P^{\prime}\cap edge(G^{\prime}(\eta_{\alpha}))
is labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}
. Thus
(\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})\in\pi({\bf Q})
and
{\bf Q}^{\prime}=(\mu_{G^{\prime}_{[l]}}P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
. By Proposition
7.17
(3), we have
\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime};G^{\prime}_{[l]})=\Omega(P;G_{l})
.

Case 2.
{\bf Q}=(P,\Delta_{j-1}(q))
. Since all
\alpha
-mutable edges of
P
are labeled
\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{4}
, this case can be proved similar to the case that
{\bf P}=P
.

The situation that
{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
can be proved similarly to situation that
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
by using Proposition
7.17
(3) and Lemmas
4.14
,
4.15
,
4.16
.

\square

The case that
\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k-1}
or
\alpha_{k+1}
need more careful discussion.

Theorem 10.3
.

Under the
Assumption
, assume that
{\bf P}
covers
{\bf Q}
and related by
\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
with
\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k-1}
or
\alpha_{k+1}
, without loss of generality, we may assume
k=1
and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
.
Then one of the following holds:

(i)

there exist
{\bf P}_{0},{\bf Q}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}
and
{\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf R}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
satisfying

(1)

{\bf P}_{0}
and
{\bf P}
are related by a sequence of twists at
\alpha
;

(2)

{\bf Q}_{0}
and
{\bf Q}
are related by a sequence of twists at
\alpha
;

(3)

{\bf P}_{0}
covers
{\bf Q}_{0}
and related by
\alpha_{1}
;

(4)

{\bf P}^{\prime}={\bf P}_{0}(0,\cdots,0)
or
{\bf P}_{0}(1,\cdots,1)
and
{\bf Q}^{\prime}={\bf Q}_{0}(0,\cdots,0)
or
{\bf Q}_{0}(1,\cdots,1)
;

(5)

{\bf P}_{0}={\bf P}^{\prime}(0,\cdots,0)
or
{\bf P}^{\prime}(1,\cdots,1)
and
{\bf Q}_{0}={\bf Q}^{\prime}(0,\cdots,0)
or
{\bf Q}^{\prime}(1,\cdots,1)
;

(6)

{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf R}^{\prime}
and
{\bf R}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
.

Moreover, we have

\Omega({\bf P}_{0},{\bf Q}_{0})=\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf R}^{\prime})+\Omega({\bf R}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}).

(ii)

there exist
{\bf P}_{0},{\bf R}_{0},{\bf Q}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}
and
{\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
satisfying

(1)

{\bf P}_{0}
and
{\bf P}
are related by a sequence of twists at
\alpha
;

(2)

{\bf Q}_{0}
and
{\bf Q}
are related by a sequence of twists at
\alpha
;

(3)

{\bf P}_{0}
covers
{\bf R_{0}}
and
{\bf R}_{0}
covers
{\bf Q}_{0}
;

(4)

{\bf P}^{\prime}={\bf P}_{0}(0,\cdots,0)
or
{\bf P}_{0}(1,\cdots,1)
and
{\bf Q}^{\prime}={\bf Q}_{0}(0,\cdots,0)
or
{\bf Q}_{0}(1,\cdots,1)
;

(5)

{\bf P}_{0}={\bf P}^{\prime}(0,\cdots,0)
or
{\bf P}^{\prime}(1,\cdots,1)
and
{\bf Q}_{0}={\bf Q}^{\prime}(0,\cdots,0)
or
{\bf Q}^{\prime}(1,\cdots,1)
;

(6)

{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
.

Moreover, we have

\Omega({\bf P}_{0},{\bf R}_{0})+\Omega({\bf R}_{0},{\bf Q}_{0})=\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}).

Proof.

We first consider the situation that
{\bf P}=P
. Assume that
Q=\mu_{G_{l}}P
and the conditions in Proposition
7.18
hold.

In case
(m_{k}(P),m_{k+1}(P))=(-1,1)
, do twists for
P
on the tiles
G_{a}
with diagonal labeled
\alpha
and
P\cap Edge(G_{a})
are labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{4}
, we obtain a perfect matching
P_{0}
. Thus all
\alpha
-twist-able edges in
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
are labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}
. Since
m_{k}(P)=-1
, we have
\tau_{i_{l-1}}=\alpha
. We see that
Q
can twist on these tiles
G_{a}
. Do the same twists for
Q
on these
G_{a}
, we obtain
Q_{0}
. Therefore,
Q_{0}=\mu_{G_{l}}P_{0}<P_{0}
.
Let
Q^{\prime}=Q_{0}(1,\cdots,1)
. By Proposition
7.18
, we have
P^{\prime}=\mu_{G^{\prime}(k+1)}\mu_{G^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}\in\varphi^{T^{o}}_{\alpha}(P_{0})
. Since all
\alpha
-twist-able edges in
P_{0}
are labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}
and all
\alpha^{\prime}
-twist-able edges in
P^{\prime}
are labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}
, we have
P^{\prime}=P_{0}(1,\cdots,1)
. Let
R^{\prime}=\mu_{G^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}
. By Proposition
7.18
(1) (c), we have
\Omega(P_{0},Q_{0})=\Omega(P^{\prime},R^{\prime})+\Omega(R^{\prime},Q^{\prime}).

The case
(m_{k}(P),m_{k+1}(P))=(0,0)
is similar to the case that
(m_{k}(P),m_{k+1}(P))=(-1,1)
as
(m_{k}(Q),m_{k+1}(Q))=(1,-1)
.

In case
(m_{k}(P),m_{k+1}(P))=(0,1)
, do twist for
P
on the tiles
G_{a}
with diagonal labeled
\alpha
such that
P\cap Edge(G_{a})
are labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{4}
, we obtain a perfect matching
P_{0}
. Thus all
\alpha
-twist-able edges in
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
are labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}
. Since
m_{k}(P)=0
, we see that
Q
can twist on these tiles
G_{a}
. Do the same twists for
Q
on these
G_{a}
, we obtain
Q_{0}
. Therefore,
Q_{0}=\mu_{G_{l}}P_{0}<P_{0}
.
Let
P^{\prime}=P_{0}(1,\cdots,1)
. Then
P^{\prime}>\mu_{G^{\prime}(k+1)}P^{\prime}
. By Proposition
7.18
,
\mu_{G^{\prime}(k+1)}P^{\prime}
can twist on
G^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}:=\mu_{G^{\prime}}\mu_{G^{\prime}(k+1)}P^{\prime}\in\varphi^{T^{o}}_{\alpha}(Q_{0})
. Since all
\alpha
-twist-able edges in
Q_{0}
are labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}
and all
\alpha^{\prime}
-twist-able edges in
Q^{\prime}
are labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}
, we have
Q^{\prime}=Q_{0}(1,\cdots,1)
. Let
R^{\prime}=\mu_{G^{\prime}(k+1)}P^{\prime}
. By Proposition
7.18
(1) (c), we have
\Omega(P_{0},Q_{0})=\Omega(P^{\prime},R^{\prime})+\Omega(R^{\prime},Q^{\prime}).

We then consider the situation that
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
. We have the following two cases:
{\bf Q}=(P,\Delta_{j-1}(q))
or
(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
.

Case 1.
{\bf Q}=(P,\Delta_{j-1}(q))
. Since
T^{\prime}
contains no arc tagged notched at
q
, we have
\tau_{[j-1]}(q)=\tau_{[j]}(q)=\alpha,\tau_{j-2}(q)=\alpha_{3},\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha_{4}
. Do twist for
P
on the tiles
G_{a}(\neq G_{c})
with diagonal labeled
\alpha
such that
P\cap Edge(G_{a})

\alpha_{1},\alpha_{4}

P_{0}

and

, we have
{\bf P}_{0}
and
{\bf P}
are related by a sequence of twists at
\alpha
,
{\bf Q}_{0}
and
{\bf Q}
are related by a sequence of twists at

and
{\bf P}_{0}
covers
{\bf Q}_{0}
. Let

and

If

and

and

. Therefore,
\Delta^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}-1}(q)=(\alpha_{1},\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{1})
. Let

-twist-able edges in

(except for the edges in

) are labeled

and

and

-pair, we have

and
{\bf Q}_{0}={\bf Q}^{\prime}(0,\cdots,0)
.

Otherwise, we have
\Delta^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}(q)=(\alpha_{4},\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{3})
with

and
{\bf Q}^{\prime}_{0}=(P^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}-2}(q))
with

and

. Thus
\Delta^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}-1}(q)=(\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1})
. Let

-twist-able edges in

are labeled

In both cases, one see that

iff

. We have

by Proposition
7.18
(4).

Case 2.

hold since the other cases can be proved similarly.

.

Case 2.1.2. We then assume that

11.
Two equivalent relations in
\mathcal{L}
.

Assumption
: We always assume that
T
and
T^{\prime}
contain no arc tagged notched at
q
in case
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
,
T
and
T^{\prime}
contain no arc tagged notched at
p
or
q
in case
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(p,q)})
if there is no other state.

In this section, we introduce two equivalent relations in
\mathcal{L}
and show any two elements of
\mathcal{L}
are equivalent.

For any
{\bf P}\in\mathcal{L}
, we denote
\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})_{\pm}
the maximum/minimum element in
\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})
and
\pi({\bf P})_{\pm}
the maximum/minimum element in
\pi({\bf P})
.

Lemma 11.1
.

For any
{\bf P}\in\mathcal{L}
, we have

w(\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P}_{+}))=w(\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P}_{-})),\hskip 14.22636ptw(\pi({\bf P}_{+}))=w(\pi({\bf P}_{-})).

Proof.

It follows by Lemma
9.23
.

\square

Definition 11.2
.

For any
{\bf P},{\bf Q}\in\mathcal{L}
,

(1)

we say that
{\bf P}
and
{\bf Q}
are
y
-equivalent
, denoted as
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
, if in
\mathbb{P}
,

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})}y^{T}({\bf R})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}=\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf Q})}y^{T}({\bf R})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf Q})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}.

(2)

we say that
{\bf P}
and
{\bf Q}
are
w
-equivalent
, denoted as
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
, if

w(\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})_{\pm})-w(\pi({\bf P})_{\pm})=w(\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf Q})_{\pm})-w(\pi({\bf Q})_{\pm}).

It is clear that
\sim
,
\approx
are equivalence relations on
\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
.

Lemma 11.3
.

For any
{\bf P}\in\mathcal{L}
such that
r=\sum m_{i}({\bf P})\geq 0
, for
{\bf P}^{\prime}={\bf P}(\vec{c})\in\pi({\bf P})
corresponding to some
\vec{c}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
as in Theorem
9.22
.
Then in
\mathbb{P}
we have

(1)

\bigoplus_{{\bf Q}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})=y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{r}
if
\vec{c}=(0,0,\cdots,0)
.

(2)

\bigoplus_{{\bf Q}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})=y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{o}}_{\alpha})^{r}
if
\vec{c}=(1,1,\cdots,1)
.

Summarize Lemmas
6.4
,
6.7
,
6.8
,
6.11
,
6.12
and
6.13
, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 11.4
.

Assume that
{\bf P}
covers
{\bf Q}
and related by
\tau
. Then we have

\frac{y^{T}({\bf Q})}{y^{T}({\bf P})}=y^{T^{o}}_{\tau}.

The main result in this section is the following.

Theorem 11.5
.

For any
{\bf P},{\bf Q}\in\mathcal{L}
, we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

In the rest of this section, we give the proof of Theorem
11.5

Lemma 11.6
.

Assume that
{\bf P}
covers
{\bf Q}
and related by
\tau
. If
\tau\neq\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
, then we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

Proof.

As
\tau\neq\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}
, we have
\sum m_{i}({\bf P})=\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\sum m_{i}({\bf P})=\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})\geq 0
. Then we have

\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})=\{{\bf P}\}
and
\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf Q})=\{{\bf Q}\}
by Theorem
9.22
. Denote
{\bf P}(0,\cdots,0)={\bf P}^{\prime}
and
{\bf Q}(0,\cdots,0)={\bf Q}^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
11.4
, we have

(25)

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})}y^{T}({\bf R})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf Q})}y^{T}({\bf R})}=\frac{y^{T}({\bf P})}{y^{T}({\bf Q})}=y^{T^{o}}_{\tau}.

In case
\tau\neq\alpha
, by Theorem
10.1
(1),
{\bf P}^{\prime}
covers
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
and related by
\tau
, and

(26)

\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q})=\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}).

By Lemmas
11.3
and
11.4
, we have

(27)

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf Q})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}=\frac{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum m_{i}({\bf P})}}{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})}}=y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\tau}.

Then
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
follows by (
25
), (
27
) and Lemma
6.3
(1).

By (
26
),
w({\bf P})-w({\bf Q})=w({\bf P}^{\prime})-w({\bf Q}^{\prime})=w({\bf P}(0,\cdots,0))-w({\bf Q}(0,\cdots,0))
. Thus
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

In case
\tau=\alpha
, by Theorem
10.1
(2),
{\bf P}^{\prime}
is covered
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
and related by
\alpha^{\prime}
, and

(28)

\Omega({\bf P},{\bf Q})=-\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime}).

By Lemmas
11.3
and
11.4
, we have

(29)

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf Q})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}=\frac{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum m_{i}({\bf P})}}{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})}}=y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}.

Then
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
follows by (
25
), (
29
) and Lemma
6.3
(2).

By (
28
),
w({\bf P})-w({\bf Q})=w({\bf P}^{\prime})-w({\bf Q}^{\prime})=w({\bf P}(0,\cdots,0))-w({\bf Q}(0,\cdots,0))
. Thus
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

The proof is complete.

\square

Lemma 11.7
.

Assume that
{\bf P}
covers
{\bf Q}
and related by
\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
with
\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k-1}
or
\alpha_{k+1}
. Then we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

Proof.

We may assume that
k=1
and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
.

We shall only consider the statement (i) of Theorem
10.3
holds, as it can be proved similarly if the statement (ii) holds.

Let
{\bf P}_{0}
,
{\bf Q}_{0}
,
{\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf R}^{\prime}
and
{\bf Q}^{\prime}
be the elements given in Theorem
10.3
. Then
{\bf P}_{0}
and
{\bf P}
are related by a sequence of twists at
\alpha
. By Lemma
11.6
we have
{\bf P}_{0}\sim{\bf P},{\bf P}_{0}\approx{\bf P}
. Similarly,
{\bf Q}_{0}\sim{\bf Q},{\bf Q}_{0}\approx{\bf Q}
. Thus it suffices to prove
{\bf P}_{0}\sim{\bf Q}_{0}
and
{\bf P}_{0}\approx{\bf Q}_{0}
.

From Theorem
10.3
, we have
\Omega({\bf P}_{0},{\bf Q}_{0})=\Omega({\bf P}^{\prime},{\bf R}^{\prime})+\Omega({\bf R}^{\prime},{\bf Q}^{\prime})
. Thus,
w({\bf P}_{0})-w({\bf Q}_{0})=w({\bf P}^{\prime})-w({\bf Q}^{\prime})
. Therefore,
{\bf P}_{0}\approx{\bf Q}_{0}
follows by Theorem
10.3
(i) (4), (5).

We now prove
{\bf P}_{0}\sim{\bf Q}_{0}
.

As
{\bf P}_{0}
covers
{\bf Q}_{0}
and related by
\alpha_{1}
, we see that
\sum m_{i}({\bf P}_{0})-\sum m_{i}({\bf Q}_{0})=b^{T^{o}}_{\alpha\alpha_{1}}=0
. We may assume that
\sum m_{i}({\bf P}_{0})=\sum m_{i}({\bf Q}_{0})\geq 0
. Then

\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P}_{0})=\{{\bf P}_{0}\}
and
\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf Q}_{0})=\{{\bf Q}_{0}\}
by Theorem
9.22
.

By Lemma
11.4
, we have

(30)

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P}_{0})}y^{T}({\bf R})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf Q}_{0})}y^{T}({\bf R})}=\frac{y^{T}({\bf P}_{0})}{y^{T}({\bf Q}_{0})}=y^{T^{o}}_{\alpha_{1}}.

By Theorem
10.3
and Lemmas
11.3
,
11.4
, we have

(31)

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf S}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P}_{0})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf S}^{\prime})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf S}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf Q}_{0})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf S}^{\prime})}=\frac{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})}{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})}=\frac{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})}{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}\frac{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})}=y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha_{1}}y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}.

As
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{1}
is the radius of the self-folded triangle
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1},\alpha^{\prime})
in
T^{\prime o}
. By Lemma
6.3
(3) (b),
y^{T^{o}}_{\alpha_{1}}=y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha_{1}}y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Thus,
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
follows by (
30
), (
31
).

The proof is complete.

\square

Lemma 11.8
.

Let
{\bf P}=P,(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
or
(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
. Assume that
{\bf P}
covers some
{\bf Q}
and related by
\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
with
\alpha_{k}\neq\alpha_{k-1},\alpha_{k+1}
. If all the
\alpha
-mutable edges of
P
are labeled
\alpha_{k-1}
or
\alpha_{k+1}
, then we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

Proof.

We may assume that
k=1
. Then
\sum m_{i}({\bf P})-\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})=b^{T^{o}}_{\alpha\alpha_{1}}>0
. We may further assume that
\sum m_{i}({\bf P})\geq 0
. Then we have

\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})=\{{\bf P}\}
by Theorem
9.22
. Denote
{\bf P}(0,\cdots,0)={\bf P}^{\prime}
and
{\bf Q}(0,\cdots,0)={\bf Q}^{\prime}
.

We divide the proof into two parts according to the conditions in Theorem
10.2
(1).

1) The cases except for the exceptional cases in Theorem
10.2
(1).

As
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}
, by Theorem
10.2
(1), we see that

w({\bf P}^{\prime}(1,\cdots,1))-w({\bf Q}^{\prime}(1,\cdots,1))=w({\bf P}(0,\cdots,0))-w({\bf Q}(0,\cdots,0)).

Thus
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

We now show that
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
.

We first consider the case that
\sum m_{i}({\bf P})\geq\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})\geq 0
. Then by Theorem
9.22

\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf Q})=\{{\bf Q}\}
.

By Lemma
11.4
, we have

(32)

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})}y^{T}({\bf R})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf Q})}y^{T}({\bf R})}=\frac{y^{T}({\bf P})}{y^{T}({\bf Q})}=y^{T^{o}}_{\alpha_{1}}.

By Lemmas
11.3
and
11.4
, we have

(33)

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf Q})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}=\frac{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum m_{i}({\bf P})}}{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})}}=y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha_{1}}(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{b_{\alpha\alpha_{1}}^{T^{o}}}.

By (
32
), (
33
) and Lemma
6.3
(3) (a), we obtain
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
.

We then consider the case that
\sum m_{i}({\bf P})\geq 0>\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})
. By Theorem
9.22
, we have
\pi({\bf Q})=\{{\bf Q}^{\prime}\}
.

By Lemmas
11.3
and
11.4
, we have

(34)

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})}y^{T}({\bf R})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf Q})}y^{T}({\bf R})}=\frac{y^{T}({\bf P})}{y^{T}({\bf Q})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{-\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})}}=y^{T^{o}}_{\alpha_{1}}\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum m_{i}({\bf Q})}.

By Lemmas
11.3
and
11.4
, we have

(35)

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf R}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf Q})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf R}^{\prime})}=\frac{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})\cdot(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum m_{i}({\bf P})}}{y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})}=y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha_{1}}(1\oplus y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum m_{i}({\bf P})}.

By (
34
), (
35
) and Lemma
6.3
(3) (a), we obtain
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
.

2) The exceptional cases in Theorem
10.2
(1).

Case 1.
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q)),{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
such that
\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha
and
(P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
{\bf P}
. By Lemma
4.10
, we have
(P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
and
(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
{\bf Q}
. By the previous discussion, we have
(P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))\star\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
for
\star\in\{\sim,\approx\}
. Since
\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha
, we have
(P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))\star{\bf P}
and
(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))\star{\bf Q}
for
\star\in\{\sim,\approx\}
by Lemma
11.6
. Therefore, we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

The remaining exceptional cases in Theorem
10.2
(1) can be proved similarly to Case 1 by using Lemmas
4.17
,
4.18
,
4.19
and
4.20
.

The proof is complete.

\square

Lemma 11.9
.

Assume that
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
(resp.
(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
) covers
{\bf Q}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
(resp.
(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
) with
\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. Then we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

Proof.

We may assume that
k=1
. We can do twists on the tiles with diagonals labeled
\alpha
for
P
to obtain a perfect matching
R
such that all
\alpha
-mutable edges are labeled
\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{4}
. We obtain a sequence of perfect matchings
P=P_{0}<P_{1}<\cdots<P_{n}=R
such that
P_{\ell}
covers
P_{\ell-1}
for all
\ell=1,\cdots,n
.

We first consider the case that
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
. By Lemma
4.10
, we have
(P_{\ell},\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
(P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j+1}(q))
,
(P_{\ell},\Delta_{j}(q))
covers
(P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j}(q))
for all
\ell
with
1\leq\ell\leq n
and
(R,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
covers
(R,\Delta_{j}(q))
. Thus by Lemmas
11.6
,
11.8
and
11.7
, for
\star\in\{\sim,\approx\}
, we obtain

{\bf P}\star(R,\Delta_{j+1}(q))\star(R,\Delta_{j}(q))\star{\bf Q}.

The case that
{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j+1}(q))
can be proved similarly by Lemmas
4.19
,
11.6
,
11.8
and
11.7
.

\square

The following result can be proved similarly by Lemmas
4.20
,
11.6
,
11.7
and
11.8
.

Lemma 11.10
.

Assume that
{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i+1}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
covers
{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
with
\tau_{j}(q)=\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. Then we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

Lemma 11.11
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta})
. Assume that
P\in\mathcal{L}
can twist on
G_{l}
such that
P
covers
Q=\mu_{G_{l}}P
. If
\tau_{i_{l}}=\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, then we have
P\sim Q
and
P\approx Q
.

Proof.

We may assume that
k=1
. Then we have
\alpha_{1}=\tau_{i_{l}}
. By Lemma
11.7
, it suffices to consider the case that
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}
. We can do twists on the tiles with diagonals labeled
\alpha
for
P
to obtain a perfect matching
R
such that all
\alpha
-mutable edges are labeled
\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{4}
. We obtain a sequence of perfect matchings
P=P_{0}<P_{1}<\cdots<P_{n}=R
such that
P_{\ell}
covers
P_{\ell-1}
for all
\ell=1,\cdots,n
.

As
P>\mu_{G_{l}}P
, we see that
P_{\ell}
can twist on
G_{l}
and
P_{\ell}>\mu_{G_{l}}P
for any
\ell\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
. Thus
R
covers
\mu_{G_{l}}R
in
\mathcal{L}
.
For any
\star\in\{\sim,\approx\}
, we have
P\star R
and
\mu_{G_{l}}P\star\mu_{G_{l}}R
by Lemma
11.6
and
R\star\mu_{G_{l}}R
by Lemma
11.8
.

Therefore,
P\sim Q=\mu_{G_{l}}P
and
P\approx Q=\mu_{G_{l}}P
.

\square

The following proposition follows immediately by Lemmas
11.6
and
11.11
.

Proposition 11.12
.

For any
{\bf P},{\bf Q}\in\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta})
, we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

We now consider the case that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
.

The following is a corollary of Lemmas
11.6
and
11.9
.

Corollary 11.13
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
. For any
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{i}(q))
and
{\bf Q}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
, we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

Lemma 11.14
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
.
If
{\bf P}=(P,\Delta_{j}(q))
covers
{\bf Q}=(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
with
\tau_{i_{l}}=\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, then
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

Proof.

We may assume that
k=1
. By Lemma
11.7
, it suffices to consider the case that
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}
. We can do twists on the tiles with diagonals labeled
\alpha
for
P
to obtain a perfect matching
R
such that all
\alpha
-mutable edges are labeled
\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{4}
. We obtain a sequence of perfect matchings
P=P_{0}<P_{1}<\cdots<P_{n}=R
such that
P_{\ell}
covers
P_{\ell-1}
for all
\ell=1,\cdots,n
. From the proof of Lemma
11.11
,
R
can twist on
G_{l}
.

We first prove that

(36)

(P,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(R,\Delta_{j}(q)),\;\;\;\;(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\mu_{G_{l}}R,\Delta_{j}(q))

for
\star=\sim,\approx
. It suffices to prove that, for all
\ell=1,\cdots,n
,

(P_{\ell},\Delta_{j}(q))\star(P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j}(q)),\;\;\;\;\;(\mu_{G_{l}}P_{\ell},\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\mu_{G_{l}}P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j}(q)).

As
T
contains no arc tagged notched at
q
, we have
|\Delta(T^{o},q)|\geq 2
, it implies
\Delta_{2}(q)\in\Delta(T^{o},q)
. For any
\ell\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, by Lemma
4.4
, we have
(P_{\ell},\Delta_{2}(q))
covers
(P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{2}(q))
. Therefore, by Lemma
11.6
and Corollary
11.13
, we have

(P_{\ell},\Delta_{j}(q))\star(P_{\ell},\Delta_{2}(q))\star(P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{2}(q))\star(P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j}(q)).

Similarly,
(\mu_{G_{l}}P_{\ell},\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\mu_{G_{l}}P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j}(q))
for all
\ell=1,\cdots,n
.

We then prove that
(R,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\mu_{G_{l}}R,\Delta_{j}(q))
. As
T
and
T^{\prime}
contain no arc tagged notched at
q
, we can choose
\Delta_{\ell}(q)
such that
m(\Delta_{\ell}(q);\alpha)\geq 0
. Then by Lemma
11.8
and Corollary
11.13
, we have

(37)

(R,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(R,\Delta_{\ell}(q))\star(\mu_{G_{l}}R,\Delta_{\ell}(q))\star(\mu_{G_{l}}R,\Delta_{j}(q)).

Therefore, we obtain
(P,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
by (
36
) (
37
).

\square

The following proposition follows immediately by Lemmas
11.6
,
11.9
and
11.14
.

Proposition 11.15
.

For any
{\bf P},{\bf Q}\in\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(q)})
, we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

We now turn to the case that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(p,q)})
.

The following is a corollary of Lemmas
11.6
,
11.9
and
11.10
.

Corollary 11.16
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(p,q)})
.
For any
{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
and
{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i^{\prime}}(p),P,\Delta_{j^{\prime}}(q))
, we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

Lemma 11.17
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(T^{o},\widetilde{\beta}^{(p,q)})
.
If
{\bf P}=(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))
covers
{\bf Q}=(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
with
\tau_{i_{l}}=\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, then
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and
{\bf P}\approx{\bf Q}
.

Proof.

We may assume that
k=1
. By Lemma
11.7
, it suffices to consider the case that
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}
. We can do twists on the tiles with diagonals labeled
\alpha
from
P
to obtain a perfect matching
R
such that all
\alpha
-mutable edges are labeled
\alpha_{2}
or
\alpha_{4}
. We obtain a sequence of perfect matchings
P=P_{0}<P_{1}<\cdots<P_{n}=R
such that
P_{\ell}
covers
P_{\ell-1}
for all
\ell=1,\cdots,n
.

We first prove that

(38)

(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\Delta_{i}(p),R,\Delta_{j}(q)),\;\;\;\;(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}R,\Delta_{j}(q))

for
\star=\sim,\approx
. It suffices to prove that, for all
\ell=1,\cdots,n
,

(\Delta_{i}(p),P_{\ell},\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\Delta_{i}(p),P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j}(q)),\;\;\;\;\;(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P_{\ell},\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j}(q)).

As
T
contains no arc tagged notched at
p
or
q
, we have
|\Delta(T^{o},p)|,|\Delta(T^{o},q)|\geq 2
, it implies
\Delta_{2}(p)\in\Delta(T^{o},p),\Delta_{2}(q)\in\Delta(T^{o},q)
. For any
\ell\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, by Lemma
4.11
, we have
(\Delta_{2}(p),P_{\ell},\Delta_{2}(q))
covers
(\Delta_{2}(p),P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{2}(q))
. Therefore, by Lemma
11.6
and Corollary
11.16
, we have

(\Delta_{i}(p),P_{\ell},\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\Delta_{2}(p),P_{\ell},\Delta_{2}(q))\star(\Delta_{2}(p),P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{2}(q))\star(\Delta_{i}(p),P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j}(q)).

Similarly,
(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P_{\ell},\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P_{\ell-1},\Delta_{j}(q))
for all
\ell=1,\cdots,n
.

We then prove that
(\Delta_{i}(p),R,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}R,\Delta_{j}(q))
. As
T
and
T^{\prime}
contain no arc tagged notched at
q
, we can choose
\Delta_{\ell}(p),\Delta_{\ell^{\prime}}(q)
such that
m(\Delta_{\ell}(p);\alpha),m(\Delta_{\ell^{\prime}}(q);\alpha)\geq 0
. Then by Lemma
11.8
and Corollary
11.16
, we have

(39)

(\Delta_{i}(p),R,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\Delta_{\ell}(p),R,\Delta_{\ell^{\prime}}(q))\star(\Delta_{\ell}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}R,\Delta_{\ell^{\prime}}(q))\star(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}R,\Delta_{j}(q)).

Therefore, we obtain
(\Delta_{i}(p),P,\Delta_{j}(q))\star(\Delta_{i}(p),\mu_{G_{l}}P,\Delta_{j}(q))
by (
38
) (
39
).

\square

and Corollary

.

.

For any

, we have
{\bf P}\sim{\bf Q}
and

.

It follows by Propositions

,

and

.

12.
Proof for the expansion formulas

Let
x_{0},x_{1}
be two variables such that
x_{0}x_{1}=v^{2\lambda}x_{1}x_{0}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}
. Denote
\Lambda(x_{0},x_{1})=-\Lambda(x_{1},x_{0})=\lambda
and
\Lambda(x_{0},x_{0})=\Lambda(x_{1},x_{1})=0
. Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
{\vec{c}}=(c_{1},\cdots,c_{r})\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, denote

(40)

\lambda({\vec{c}})=\sum_{i<j}\Lambda(x_{c_{i}},x_{c_{j}}),\;\;\;\;x^{\vec{c}}=v^{-\lambda({\vec{c}})}x_{c_{1}}x_{c_{2}}\cdots x_{c_{r}}.

Thus, we have

(41)

(x_{0}+x_{1})^{r}=\sum_{{\vec{c}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}v^{\lambda({\vec{c}})}x^{\vec{c}}.

We have the following observation.

Lemma 12.1
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

For any
i
with
1\leq i\leq r
, given
{\vec{c},\vec{c}\hskip 1.5pt^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
with
c_{i}=1,c^{\prime}_{i}=0
and
c_{j}=c^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\neq i
, we have

\lambda({\vec{c}})-\lambda({\vec{c}\hskip 1.5pt^{\prime}})=(2i-r-1)\lambda.

(2)

For any
i
with
1\leq i\leq n-1
, given
{\vec{c},\vec{c}\hskip 1.5pt^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
with
c_{i}=c^{\prime}_{i+1}=0,c_{i+1}=c^{\prime}_{i}=1
and
c_{j}=c^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\neq i,i+1
, we have

\lambda({\vec{c}})-\lambda({\vec{c}\hskip 1.5pt^{\prime}})=2\lambda.

Proof.

(1) As
c_{i}=1,c^{\prime}_{i}=0
and
c_{j}=c^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\neq i
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda({\vec{c}})-\lambda({\vec{c}\hskip 1.5pt^{\prime}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{s<t}\Lambda(x_{c_{s}},x_{c_{t}})-\sum_{s<t}\Lambda(x_{c^{\prime}_{s}},x_{c^{\prime}_{t}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{s<i}(\Lambda(x_{c_{s}},x_{1})-\Lambda(x_{c_{s}},x_{0}))+\sum_{i<s}(\Lambda(x_{c_{s}},x_{1})-\Lambda(x_{c_{s}},x_{0}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(i-1)\lambda-(r-i)\lambda

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(2i-r-1)\lambda.

(2) As
c_{i}=c^{\prime}_{i+1}=0,c_{i+1}=c^{\prime}_{i}=1
and
c_{j}=c^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\neq i,i+1
,

\displaystyle\lambda({\vec{c}})-\lambda({\vec{c}\hskip 1.5pt^{\prime}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{s<t}\Lambda(x_{c_{s}},x_{c_{t}})-\sum_{s<t}\Lambda(x_{c^{\prime}_{s}},x_{c^{\prime}_{t}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Lambda(x_{0},x_{1})-\Lambda(x_{1},x_{0})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2\lambda.

\square

Throughout this section, denote by
b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
the
\alpha^{\prime}
-th column of the extended exchange matrix
\widetilde{B}(T^{\prime o})
and
(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{\pm}
are the positive and negative part, respectively, of
b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 12.2
.

\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf Q}\in\pi^{\prime}\pi({\bf P})}y^{T}({\bf Q})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf Q}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q^{\prime}})}=\frac{\bigoplus_{{\bf Q}\in\mathcal{L}}y^{T}({\bf Q})}{\bigoplus_{{\bf Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q^{\prime}})}.

Proposition 12.3
.

For any
{\bf P}\in\mathcal{L}
with
r=\sum m_{i}({\bf P})\geq 0

X^{T}({\bf P})|_{v=1}=\sum_{{\bf P}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}X^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})|_{v=1}.

Proof.

As
r\geq 0
, we have that in the cluster algebra

y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}(\vec{c}))=y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}(0,\cdots,0))(y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum_{i}c_{i}}.

Therefore, by Definitions

,
6.6
,

, we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&\sum_{\vec{c}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}X^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}(\vec{c}))|_{v=1}\\
&=&\sum_{\vec{c}}\frac{M\cdot(x_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{-r}\cdot(x_{\alpha_{2}}x_{\alpha_{4}})^{r-\sum_{i}c_{i}}\cdot(x_{\alpha_{1}}x_{\alpha_{3}})^{\sum_{i}c_{i}}\cdot y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}(0,\cdots,0))(y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum_{i}c_{i}}}{\oplus_{{\bf Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})}\\
&=&M\cdot(x_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{-r}\cdot y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}(0,\cdots,0))\frac{\textstyle\sum_{\vec{c}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}(x_{\alpha_{2}}x_{\alpha_{4}})^{r-\sum c_{i}}\cdot(x_{\alpha_{1}}x_{\alpha_{3}}y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{\sum c_{i}}}{\bigoplus_{{\bf Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})}\\
&=&M\cdot(x_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{-r}\cdot y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}(0,\cdots,0))\frac{(x_{\alpha_{2}}x_{\alpha_{4}}+x_{\alpha_{1}}x_{\alpha_{3}}y^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})^{r}}{\bigoplus_{{\bf Q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}y^{T^{\prime}}({\bf Q}^{\prime})},\\
\end{array}

By Lemma

(4),

. Thus we have

By Lemma

, we obtain

Then the result follows by (

) (

) and Proposition

Lemma 12.7
.

For any
{\bf P}\in\mathcal{L}
with
r=\sum m_{i}({\bf P})\geq 0
, the following are equivalent:

w({\bf P})=w({\bf P}(0,\cdots,0))
,

Proof.

, we may write

. Hence the coordinates of

and

in

(X^{T})^{\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}}=(X^{T^{\prime}})^{\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}}
.

Thus

(44)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&X^{T}({\bf P})=(X^{T})^{re_{\alpha}+\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}}=v^{-\Lambda^{T}(\overrightarrow{{\bf P}},re_{\alpha})}(X^{T})^{\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}}\cdot(X^{T})^{re_{\alpha}}\\
&=&v^{-\Lambda^{T}(\overrightarrow{{\bf P}},re_{\alpha})}(X^{T^{\prime}})^{\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}}\cdot\left((X^{T^{\prime}})^{-e_{\alpha^{\prime}}+(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{+}}+(X^{T^{\prime}})^{-e_{\alpha^{\prime}}+(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{-}}\right)^{re_{\alpha}}\\
&=&v^{-\Lambda^{T}(\overrightarrow{{\bf P}},re_{\alpha})}(X^{T^{\prime}})^{\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}}\cdot\textstyle\sum_{\vec{c}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}v^{\lambda(\vec{c})}(X^{T^{\prime}})^{-re_{\alpha^{\prime}}+\sum c_{i}(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{-}+(r-\sum c_{i})(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{+}}\\
&=&v^{-\Lambda^{T}(\overrightarrow{{\bf P}},re_{\alpha})}v^{\Lambda^{T^{\prime}}(\overrightarrow{{\bf P}},-re_{\alpha^{\prime}}+r(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{+})}\textstyle\sum_{\vec{c}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}v^{\lambda(\vec{c})}(X^{T^{\prime}})^{\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}-re_{\alpha^{\prime}}+\sum c_{i}(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{-}+(r-\sum c_{i})(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{+}}\\
&=&\textstyle\sum_{\vec{c}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}v^{\lambda(\vec{c})}(X^{T^{\prime}})^{\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}-re_{\alpha^{\prime}}+\sum c_{i}(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{-}+(r-\sum c_{i})(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{+}},\end{array}

where the fourth equality follows by (
41
) and
\lambda(\vec{c})
is given by (
40
) under the convention that
x_{0}=(X^{T^{\prime}})^{-e_{\alpha^{\prime}}+(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{-}},x_{1}=(X^{T^{\prime}})^{-e_{\alpha^{\prime}}+(b^{T^{\prime o}}_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{+}}
.

On the other hand, by Remark
9.10
, Proposition
9.12
and Lemma

, we have

\sum_{{\bf P}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}v^{w({\bf P}^{\prime})}X^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})=\sum_{\vec{c}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}v^{w({\bf P}(\vec{c}))}(X^{T^{\prime}})^{\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}-re_{\alpha^{\prime}}+\sum c_{i}(b_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{T^{\prime o}})_{-}+(r-\sum c_{i})(b_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{T^{\prime o}})_{+}}.

By Lemma
9.23
and Lemma
12.1
(1), for any
i\in\{1,\cdots,r\}
and
{\vec{c},\vec{c}\hskip 1.5pt^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
with

and

for

by (

), we have

,
it follows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&\sum_{{\bf P}^{\prime}\in\pi({\bf P})}v^{w({\bf P}^{\prime})}X^{T^{\prime}}({\bf P}^{\prime})\\
&=&v^{w({\bf P}(0,\cdots,0))}\sum_{\vec{c}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}v^{\lambda(\vec{c})}(X^{T^{\prime}})^{\overrightarrow{{\bf P}}-re_{\alpha^{\prime}}+\sum c_{i}(b_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{T^{\prime o}})_{-}+(r-\sum c_{i})(b_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{T^{\prime o}})_{+}}\\

Then the result follows by (

) and (

).

The rest of this section is devoted to giving the proof of Theorem
6.14
.

Given a quantum cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}_{v}(\Sigma)
and a puncture
p
, recall the quantum cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}^{(p)}_{v}(\Sigma)
and the isomorphism

. Denote by

the corresponding isomorphism between

and
\mathcal{A}^{(p)}_{v}(\Sigma)\mid_{v=1}
. For a given tagged triangulation
T
, for any
\Delta_{j}(p)\in\Delta(T^{o},p)
, denote by
x^{T,(p)}(P_{\beta},\Delta_{j}(p))
,
h^{T,(p)}(P_{\beta},\Delta_{j}(p))
,

and

the weight, height monomial, specialized height monomial and quantum weight, respectively of

in

, which are given in Definition

.

We divide the proof of Theorem

into two cases:

or
